And while the son of Arhmn was dealing blows (462) to the son of Ormizd, they sought an arbiter and found none; [141] consequently they set about making the sun so that it might be an arbiter for them. There, the inventor of the means of bringing the Sun into existence, they say is formally Arhmn, and here, manifestly (463) indeed, co-creator of light. And let us admit that there was no one (there) to be an arbiter: and to their father? could they not go? or to the one to whom the father and the son offered sacrifice, according to their fable? Then now how can they well be enemies of each other, their Ormizd and their Arhmn, who, in a single bosom, had indeed been able to lie (464), and went to each other's home to take meals and, in collaboration with one another, the sun, once they had created it, established it as arbiter? Now one (of these two versions) a certain Zradašt, to favor licentiousness, puts (it) forward (as a thesis) — namely that it is by outrages done to mother and sister that sun and moon were created — so that, upon such (models) (465), its gaze once fixed, the nation, to the same impurities, without discernment, may betake itself (466). Then, the other (version), to conceal such shame, he makes (it) popular: namely that it is for the sake of arbitration that they created <soleil> (467). And because their religious beliefs are not at all under Scriptures, sometimes they say this, and with it deceive, and [142] sometimes that, and with that very thing they catch the gullible. Only, let us admit it: God, Ormizd was He; (then) it is from nothing that He had the power to create the luminaries, as (He did for) heavens and earth, and not from a depraved activity or because of the non-existence of an arbiter (468). [II. X] Again, some other thing they say still which is, at all, not believable: namely that, when <the son of> Ormizd (469) was on the point of dying, his seed, into a spring, he ejaculated; and that towards the end (of the world), from this seed a virgin must be born, and, from her, a son, once born, beats the bulk of the armies of Arhmn; then two others still alike (to the first), having come into existence in the same way, beat his armies and annihilate (them). First, by this they are refuted that water, of seed, is not preservative, but destructive. And then, from this other angle, they are rebuffed: rather than giving to the source his seed to keep alive, he himself, why did he not have the strength to keep himself alive, but, son that he was of such a good God, by the son of the evil one (470) was he slain? And it is evident that those who, when they were in the process of falling, [143] conquered the good one and his son, at the end (of times) also, they themselves, by violence, must prevail, they whose armies they (the Magi) enumerate in numbers such that they are no longer numbers (471). (To object) again: if their gods come to be subject to death, they themselves, how can they hope for resurrection? And especially the resurrection of their three Immortals (472), which must not be counted as resurrection! Only, if truly, as they say, the son (of Ormizd) is dead, neither as to (473) Ormizd, nor as to his other son Xorašet, was there legitimately any doubt that they must (also) die, since the lineage of their gods is a race (of beings) for whom being united by marriage and being subject to death are one and the same (473). Conclusion to A: Eznik restates the thesis De Deo uero And such gods, not as gods must one hold them, but as non-gods. For he who is the true God, all that is his, whatever it may be, it is in eternity that he possesses it: (namely) possessing being by essence in the same way (as he possesses) perpetually life; and (possessing) his son always with Him, without [144] occasional causes whatsoever and without mediation; and (possessing) creation not by virtue of (demiurgic) inventions, but by virtue of a power that, what it wills, it can. And he has no one contrary to himself, so that (474), he himself, of goods would be creator, and the other, of evils; as these, to Ormizd attribute the good creatures, and to Arhmn, the evil ones. B. No creature evil by nature. What they cannot show us, (475) if, keeping to certain facts, they come to look, (is) a creature in any respect evil, which, by nature, is evil, neither their Arhmn, nor devils that they attribute to him as creatures; as many times, by multiple examples, we have not failed, in the first dissertations (476), to clearly make seen. 1. a. NEITHER RATIONAL BEINGS But, supposing that, in their idea, Arhmn is evil, because he bears the name Xaraman (477), - for what he has cast outside the sun (478) those who have inflamed desires for the sun, whence, even to the very name Xaraman, he has received - in the same way, the name Satan also is by no means a name of nature, but of moral comportment; as from 'goodness' someone is called 'good', and from 'badness', 'evil', and this, not in that there are here moral comportments born from nature, but rather an adventitious (suchness) (which we, we see) (479). And from there it is illuminated (the fact) that, [145] many times, a number of incorrigibles we see having become wise, and, of the wise, having become incorrigible; of the impudent, having become chaste, and of the chaste, having become impudent. This, for what exists on the side of rational beings. 3. b. NEITHER IRRATIONAL BEASTS [II. XI] Moreover, for beings deprived of reason, for animals (480), because they differ in their habits, one must not imagine two creators, as they precisely, through foolishness, have believed that, their Ormizd, it is animals, quadrupeds and birds and fish, and all that is good and beautiful, that he created; and, their Arhmn, evil wild beasts and impure birds, and reptiles and serpents and scorpions and all noxious vermin. Supposing that heavens and earth and airs and waters are creatures of Ormizd, how is it that the noxious (animals), created as they are by Arhmn, can dwell on the earth of that one (Ormizd), and breathe the same airs, and, from the same nourishments, which come out of the earth, provision themselves, and, in the same waters, the impure vermin, with the pure fish, feed, and in the same airs, the birds that feed on carrion, [146] with the birds of nobler species, shimmer in their flight (481); (all evil beings) that the good creatures of Ormizd ought to kill and not feed, since indeed to him belong earth and waters and airs! And, supposing that, of wild beasts, on account of their noxiousness, one goes to believe that they come from some evil creator, men, it is more fitting to think that they come from an evil creator, and not those (wild beasts). For these (men) are more noxious to wild beasts, than wild beasts to these. For these (men), once out of towns and villages, become beaters to shoot down the wild beasts; and those (wild beasts), once they have fled to mountains and escarpments, have but one haste: to seek safety in flight (482). Even for half of them, the lairs are (located) outside the society of men. Likewise also for reptiles, the calling cry, only, of man, have they perceived: some, into holes, some, into hollows, some, into crevasses of the earth, there they have entered! They hide! And if, from the fact that men harass them too much, they come to harm, from this very thing, the fault is men's, and not theirs [146.26]. [147.7] Once more (483), while keeping eyes on their noxiousness, n not point harm each other! (484) For, supposing that this noxiousness, which is theirs, which, without premeditation, passes into action, should be hated, how much more still the malice of men, which, with premeditation and perfidy, will come to pass into action! (485) [147.14]. [146.26] And by that, it is not from some Evil One, [147.1] that, the wild beasts and vermin, one must think they come, but rather from a single, good creator, partly for (our) needs, and partly for ornament, and partly by way of scarecrows, to abate the unjustifiable arrogance of man [147.7]. [147.15] There is more: with beings of nothing (486) he afflicts us, as in fact (487) by flea and fly and gnat and wasp, mite and mouse, and others such, which precisely (488) are tiny creatures of nothing and can well harass us. For, of these (489), there is one that harasses us personally, and one or another that is potentially harmful to what is properly ours (490), such as: mouse, and moth, and worm, and everything that may resemble them. And, by means of these, once he has brought (it) down, he sets our spirit in a humble state; so that (491) when, (from experience), we come to know that even the small (animals) [148] have power to be potentially harmful to us, we may descend from the heights of unjustifiable pride, in order not to go and consider ourselves as beings worthy of great honors. Furthermore, in order to bring also, in addition, the solicitude of God to mind, let us reflect (492): from the moment that small things can harm us, how could we escape danger if, with reptiles and wild beasts, he had made us cohabitants! But also on this we must keep our eyes: namely, how many animals he has placed in submission under us for our needs: horses and camels and elephants and cattle and sheep; and, originating from the mountains and the plains (493), roe deer and stags and wild goats (494) and wild boars, of which half are beasts of burden and half are good to eat. And this also he has made known to us: that over those for whom he himself wills it, we may be potentially victorious; and those for whom he does not will it, we cannot conquer, not only the valuable animals, but even those that are nothing (495). What can be viler than the flea and the mouse? And over them, we can neither prevail, nor can we, nor expel them from the world. [149] And yet another is a kind of brood (496) in the waters, and will not serve our needs, but (is there) only to sting us (497). In the moment when we see that we do not have in us what is necessary to prevail, we will know the limit of our own strengths, and for good (498) we will descend from the heights of vain pride, and to him alone will we give the victory, who, by means of the (creatures) of nothing, harasses us, and subjects the very great ones to us: such as elephants and camels and lions and leopards and panthers, of which, half as beasts of burden, and half for amusements, once he has trained them, he tames. c. NOR SATAN [II. XII] It is true that others have held a different opinion concerning Satan, namely, that it is God in person who created him evil (499). If therefore it were God who had created him evil, why does the Church cast out devils? If for the purpose of exacting vengeance for evil (500), devils were established, it follows that the Church harms those who, precisely by them (the devils), are, as the case may be, admonished; and she stands against the will of God, for He created them for admonition, and she [150] casts them out! Only, that the power to cast out, she by no means holds from herself, but from God, is clearly evident from this: suppose, if the Lord had not previously breathed the Spirit upon the Twelve nor given sovereign power to the Seventy, they could not cast out devils! (501). There is more: he himself in person, if he had recognized them as admonishers (502), would not have commanded them with a threatening voice, nor ordered his disciples to cast out those whom he himself expressly would have ordered to enter into men. And how can it be that Angels, when they hear the name of God, enter into joy, and devils, never? But, even more, when they hear (it), they tremble with fear. If, to put a stop to transgressions, devils had as their office, never would they have pushed men to idolatry (503), nor to all kinds of sects of philosophers and heretics, nor to distributions (504) of destinies, nor (to distributions) inscribed as on the forehead, nor (to distributions made) by decrees bound (to the subject) (505), nor, towards the stars, to keep their eyes fixed attentively, thinking that, to some extent, they can be causes of happiness and misfortune. And that the invention of devils is [151] idolatry, David attests: "All the gods of the Gentiles are devils!" And the blessed Paul: "What agreement (506) is there of Christ with Belial? And behold indeed - as they precisely say - that there is agreement, for them, from one to the other! For, if it is because "evil" has God created him, that perchance he torments, it is not legitimate to call him "evil", but rather "He who takes vengeance". For, "evil" would he then be, if, his mandate, he did not execute." Apart from these reasons, why was it not him (Satan) but rather Angel-of-God who struck the firstborn of the Egyptians and many times the Jews over there in the desert? For, if it were he, who, to this, would have been appointed, why was it not precisely he who struck, but an Angel? And, in the days of David, the seventy thousand from the twelve tribes of Israel and, from the camp of the Assyrians, the one hundred seventy-five thousand soldiers - he who struck them, it was Angel-of-God, and not devils! Why also in the days of Jesua, son of Yovsédek, an Angel said [152] to the Slanderer who was there opposing him: "May the Lord command you imperiously (507), Satan!" Why also are the Jews called "sons of Satan", because they transgress the Law, supposing that he remains (faithful) to the order (he received), and that they really have transgressed the commandment (508)? And liar of his own self (509) why must he be called (510), he who in the truth (511) precisely must stand fast (510) in his function? For not from him is the evil, but from him who has created him (Satan) such. And on what charge is he to be sent (510) into the outer darkness? But, they say, rest is for him the darkness! Ah! This time! that is not at all what cried the legion of devils! But indeed that torments have been prepared for it! "What have we in common, we and you, said it, Son of God, that you, you, have come here before the time, to torment us?" And, to make his depravity (of Satan) evident, (the Lord) said: "No one can seize the possessions of the Strong One, unless first he binds the Strong One." Now why did he bind him, except because he knew that it is by will that he acts evilly, and that (512), when he wills it, it is in his power to become wise. [153] If, by nature, (God) had appointed him to torment, why would (the Lord) slanderously call by the name of "Evil" the one who kept his nature, (always) the same (513), exactly as it came to him with his being? Moreover, he deserved no punishments for proving by his acts the truth of his nature. For no one punishes the fire (saying to it): "Why do you burn?", nor the waters (saying to them): "Why do you choke (by drowning)?" C. Against the Decrees (astrology). And, all together, the questions that they also, again, raise about the "Decrees", are resolved in the same sense.

### QUESTION OF THE ADVERSARIES
[II. XIII] Is it by virtue of a "Decree", they say, that men must die, or without there being "Decrees"? RESPONSE First of all we must know (from them) what the "Decrees" might be, and whence the term "Decrees" could have spread through the world. a. THERE IS NO QUESTION OF IRREVOCABLE DECREES CONCERNING DEATH AND LIFE IN THE SCRIPTURES Of a "decree", tied once and for all (514) regarding death, in the Scriptures given by God, we find nowhere; because the Lord, for death and life (515), is master both of shortening the time of execution of his decree and of prolonging (it). As at the time of the flood he said: "Soient les jours de vie de ces hommes-ci de cent vingt ans et, à cause du foisonnement [154] de leur iniquité il retrancha de ces (cent vingt), les vingt. Et, comme au temps d'Adam, <dont au commencement les années n'étaient pas comptées,> (516) il dit: "Le jour où tu manges du fruit de l'arbre, le même (jour) tu meurs! Et, dans sa bienfaisance, en vue de (lui laisser le temps de) donner naissance à ses fils, et aux hommes, dans le monde, de se propager, il lui accorda par grâce neuf cent trente ans pour que point n'allât son beau modelage (516) entièrement s'effacer. Et, à cause de ses larmes, au roi Ezéchias furent ajoutés quinze ans Et, à cause de leur pénitence, les Ninivites, point ne détruisit-il la ville au troisième jour, comme l'avait publiquement annoncé son prophète b. ENSEIGNEMENT DES CHALDÉENS ASTROLOGUES a. Les astres sont causes de naissance et de mort a'. Thèse. Et point (n'enseignent-elles, les Écritures) comme les Chaldéens astrologues, qui, causes et de naissance et de mort, posent en thèse qu'il faut les tirer d'astres, comme (on ferait) d'êtres vivants. Comme si, au moment précis où gens viennent à naître, dès ce moment-là même, les morts elles aussi d'un chacun viennent infailliblement à être déterminées; et (comme si) (517), d'après cette (théorie), ni d'avancer, à personne, pour mourir (518), il ne soit possible, ni de retarder. β'. Réfutation de α' (thèse). Mais, les réfute encore ce qui arrive ès [155] guerres. Car, en un seul jour, des dizaines de milliers d'hommes meurent massacrés, d'âges tout différents: les uns en leurs tendres années; les autres, jeunes hommes; et d'autres, arrivés à parfaite taille de vieillesse; dont les naissances n'étaient point à une seule époque et les morts ont lieu toutes ensemble. And once again, if it were the stars that were to draw forth causes of births, why, among the Hindus, was no one ever born white, and why does no analogous phenomenon exist in other countries? Could it be by chance that only there (in India) the star that makes white was unable to reach, and that, not in other countries, (could reach) the star that fashions black? And the teeth of the Hindu, whence comes it that they are so white? β. Good or bad influence of such and such a celestial animal in its astral residence a'. Thesis. [II. XIV] Once again, they posit as a thesis that the stars are causes of good fortunes and misfortunes; as if certain astral residences (519) can exist, and (as if) (520), agreeing with the encounter that the causing stars (521) make upon their entry into the said astral residences, (with this encounter), they too, the men who are born, must be in a relationship of similarity (522). All the time when it is the Lion, they say, that comes to be in the astral residence, and someone comes to be born, [156] (that someone) is destined to become king; and all the time when it is the Ox (523), and someone comes to be born, strong and well-marked (524), (that someone) must come to be; and all the time when it is the Ram, and someone comes to be born, (that someone) is destined to be rich, just as that one (the Ram) is hairy and woolly; and when it is the Scorpion, and someone comes to be born, to become wicked and delinquent, (that someone) is destined. And the other (animals) (525) also, of various other things, (thus) become causes! So much so that (526) when their Kronos comes to enter into the aforesaid astral residence (527), a king dies! Which precisely took place, in all truth, twice, as they say, under the emperor Theodosius (528). And the Chaldeans had firmly asserted: "The king is at death's door!" And he did not die, so that their art of divination might be covered with shame, in its obvious falsehoods (529)! β'. Refutation of their α' (thesis). Come then, first of all, this, that they say it: who hoisted such terrestrial ⌈animals⌉ (530), eaters of corpses and eaters of grass, up to the heavens, so that they might be causes of the birth of men there? For, whoever, of whomever, can possibly (531) become a cause of birth, that one, superior to the one of whom he is the cause, must be in wisdom. [157] Now, with the eyes of common sense, let us see a little (532)! Who can be the superior here? Man, who commands the animal as master? Or the animal, who is enslaved to man? And not only is he enslaved to him, but even is his food! And, of the wild beasts, half, having become fleeing beasts as soon as (533) we see them, and half, in the thick thickets of the jungles as soon as (533) withdrawn, when they come to hear the mere voice of man. Because fear and dread of man the Creator has cast upon the wild beasts and the reptiles, and upon even the flying animals (534), to further honor him whose, until his creation, as something of a remarkable novelty, he presents to us: namely, that he formed him as it were by means of hands, and that he breathed into him breath of life, as if it were by means of mouth. And, thereby, he makes it evident that he wants to show him more worthy of honor (than the other creatures). Moreover, that God is above composite bodies, this is evident to one who is in the truth. And, if it is for him (man) that the wild beasts and the animals received existence - as the experience of things in reality shows - how could it be that they ascended to the heavens, and, [158] for men, became causes of birth, (beings) who are as far from life as is a candle which, by men, comes to have been fabricated to be a giver of light to the house during the night? And non-living as they are, how could they, for the living, become a cause of birth? But if they were not alive, they say, likewise, they would not be endowed with movement. Only, since they move, it is evident that they are alive! For the moment, let them listen carefully! Supposing that everything endowed with movement is alive, it will follow that the waters too, which move, will be counted as living; and a blazing fire, because it moves, will be believed living; and airs and winds, because they blow, will be placed among the living; and plants and shoots of grass! which, although they move slowly, yet by their growth show themselves as being endowed with movement. And therefore, just as everything that is endowed with movement does not have intellectual and rational life, likewise neither sun, nor moon, nor stars (535) have it. Indeed, the heavens too, under which they revolve, have no [159] intellectual and rational life. But indeed heavens (536) and earth are vessels holding fixed in their place, firmly founded by the Creator to hold contracted beneath Himself (537) everything that exists between the two; and the luminaries (are) like lamps lit to dispel the darkness by driving it from the midst of this great house here below (538). And they are in servitude by nature, at the command of their Creator, for the enjoyment of all living beings; and, for themselves, they themselves are not, since they have no consciousness of what "being" or "not being" might be for them (539); in the way that, both heavens and earth and waters and woods and stones, are for those for whom (540) they came into existence, and for themselves, they themselves are not; since they have no consciousness of what "being" or "not being" might be for them (541), because they are not endowed with intelligence and reason. Once again, in the way they say, that when it is the Lion that comes to be in the astral residence, it is a king that must be born, if it were so, many times many kings would have to be born. For there is not just a single individual to be born when it is the lion that comes to be in the astral residence, but many. [160] And if, truly, the Lion were the cause of the birth of kings, it would follow that no son of a king would become king, but rather (the son) of whomever, whose birth coincided with the entry of the Lion into the astral residence. In reality, if <we are willing to see> (542), we see that "son of a king" becomes "king". In the way that a son of David, Solomon, sat on the throne of his father, and the son of the same, on the throne of the same, and (of which), one after another, the succession of the kings of Judah stretched out until the Maccabees, in the same way, if it concerns the Assyrians and the Babylonians, it is "son, from father" (543) that they received the kingship by succession; as, they too, from a certain Sassan, the Sassanids, "son, from father", by succession, have received until today the kingship of the Sassanids; and there has not been found a Lion, in the heavens, to have entered the astral residence, to actually have the power to transfer the kingship to another family, somewhere, in the land of the Levant. [II. XV] Then it is evident that, just as of kingship, the star is not cau [161] se, likewise it is not, neither of strength (544), nor the efficient cause of wealth (545); all the more so, in reality, as we see rich become poor, and poor become rich. Will they perhaps, this too, be able to say, that one and the same star, of wealth and poverty, can become the cause? And of strength and weakness? For, the strong, from time to time, we see them become weak, and the weak, become strong; and the wicked, become wise, and the wise, become wicked. c. THE "BOOK OF FATE" And where can well be (in the reality of things) (546) what they formally say, that everything that in the "Book of Fate" the "Decrees" have written, to that it is not possible to fail to happen; but (that) (547), whoever has been "glorious" formally inscribed there, is glorious, and whoever, formally miserable, miserable; and (that) (547), where the "Decrees" ⌈will have ordered it⌉ (548), and by whom the "Decrees" (will have ordered it), it is in accordance with that (ordinance) that they too (men) die; and (that) (547) to defraud is not possible by evading the decree of the "Decree" (549). O powerless "Decree" and "ordinance" without force! which, both thieves and brigands can render null, when, once fallen upon someone, out of goods and out of [162] sun they come to throw him! And if it is by virtue of some "ordinance" of "Decree" that things must eventually happen, neither should kings give a decree of death, nor judges flay alive and kill the killers of men; they (kings and judges) who, by making (them) undergo these punishments, make it evident that it is not in conformity with some "ordinance of the Decrees" that the crimes of criminals ⌈take their origin⌉, but rather that they are the result of violence set to work evil (550)! Or else, when a bandit, in a country, comes to make an incursion, to rob and massacre the people, let them not (the kings) go assemble armed forces and let them not, body of troops against body of troops, organize (them), to drive the bandit out of the country! But the justifications (the reason they have for not doing it) let them give them (551): "Si... "Decrees" there are, that the country, by a bandit, be plundered, we, why should we turn against the "Decrees?" On the contrary, by massing armed forces and driving the enemy out of the country, they show clearly that it is not in conformity with some "decree of Decree" that plunderings come to be wrought, but rather by the violence of the bandit, who, having come there in a spirit of rapine, plunders the country, and [163] strips it of (its) goods and possessions. II. FREE EVIL AND DIVINE FOREKNOWLEDGE Predestination propter praeuisa merita. 1. God knows in advance the harms of which those who can harm are the cause. [II. XVI] Only, this also, must one know, that, every kind of harm, which, by those who can harm, comes to be wrought, God has foreknowledge of it. And if he knows, they say, the harms that are to come upon men, why, on the occasions, does he not prevent them? (552). Here is what it is (553): to what extent God prevents harms, by turning them away from men, that, it is not for all to have a clear view of it, but for Him alone who distributes the cares that he gives to all, according to the needs of each one. He sometimes shortens the harms themselves, caused by those who can harm, so that thus it may not seem at all (554), that he cannot prevent the harms. On the other hand, he leaves free that very one who can harm, to satisfy his will upon his neighbor, so that there may not appear (554) that, under constraint, the conduct of rational beings is done, but so that, emerging from the works of each one, what each one is personally, may come visibly to manifest itself (555). 2. The foreknowledge of God is not the cause of evils. And in advance he knows everything, and the knowledge that he has of it in advance is not the cause of evils. For, neither when [164] someone, by chance, sees his neighbor, on slippery places, having set out to walk, and comes to say: "He is going to tumble down!", this (someone), has not been in any way, the cause of the tumbling down of the said neighbor (556); nor when, in the case where it is through places infested with brigands that the neighbor has launched himself, someone comes to see this and to say: "He is in the process of finding his ruin!", this (someone), not in the least, comes to be the cause of the mischief; nor, when, in the case of some noble personage whose son is ripe for debauchery (557), he comes to see himself and to say: "He is going to lose the riches that he holds from his fathers!", this (someone) not in the least, comes to be the cause of the squandering of the riches. In the same way, neither is God's foreknowledge the cause of goods or evils. 3. God knows everything in advance, but there are things that he wants and others that he does not want. Predestination, but propter praeuisa merita. God knows everything in advance; but there are things that he wants and there are those that he does not want. He wanted to bring the flood, and it was not at all his will that by the flood, man and animal all together be slain, but, from the unworthy couplings of the sex, the provocative boldness (558) brought him to this: to do what formally he did not want; as he himself And if it is he (God) who hardened, they say, why did he strike with plagues, him (the Pharaoh) and the land of the Egyptians? But the Apostle enters here as defender of his Lord: he assures that it is not He (the Lord) who hardened the Pharaoh, but rather that it is he himself (the Pharaoh) who, of himself, (hardened) himself. And what God says there, namely: "I have hardened!" comes back approximately to this: in the way that someone - when, his neighbor or servant, he comes to greatly honor him, and that one, all puffed up with pride, comes to despise the one who made him great - (in the way, I say, that someone) comes, he too, to say: "Why do I put the fault on him? It is I, of myself, who have done injury to myself, because I promoted to honor one who does not deserve it!", in the same way, of God also, must one understand that he says there: "It is I, by my forbearance, who have been the cause of his hardening, because I did not, from the beginning, [167] slay his firstborn!" (562) Moreover, God wanted, says (the Apostle), to show his wrath and to make manifest his power which he deployed with much long-suffering upon the vessels prepared for perdition Whence it is clear that it was the long-suffering of God which was the cause of the hardening of Pharaoh's heart, for this reason, that the final plagues, from the beginning, He did not bring upon him. Moreover, that God had not turned Pharaoh's heart to stone must be inferred from this fact, that at times he consented to release the people, and at times he did not consent. And "vessels prepared for destruction" they themselves made themselves, and not God, of whom the apostle formally says that: "He wills that all men live for good and come to the knowledge of the truth for good (563). And, truly, were not they also (the Egyptians and Pharaoh) of this "all men"? 5. No accounts to be demanded of God. And it is not as the opposing party says, that: "It is not according to him who wills, nor [168] according to him who runs, but according to God who shows mercy. For on whom He wills, He shows mercy; and whom He wills, He hardens (564). And, shutting at once the mouth of the opposing party, the Blessed One says: "You, who are you, O man, who demands an answer from God? Even if in some manner it were so, you, who would you be, who would demand accounts of God? Does the clay, perchance, come to say to the potter: 'Why have you made me thus of your own accord?'" 6. Man obeys whom he wills and makes himself a useful vessel. Only, that it is not so, the same, in person, in the same letter, in writing, says (565): "To whom you will, you offer your persons, to be in submission, whether (in submission) to righteousness, or (in submission) to submission to sins (566)." And addressing Timothy in writing, he says: "If anyone keeps himself pure, he has become, that one, a vessel fully prepared, useful for the work of his Lord" And the prophet says: "On condition that you are willing to will it and that you listen to me, the good of the land you shall eat" Conclusion to 4, 5, 6. And by all this, and other (texts) in great number, it is clear that it is not He (God) who prepares vessels of wrath for destruction, nor [169] vessels of mercy for glory, but rather that they themselves (these vessels prepare) themselves either for destruction or for glory. 7. For God there is no acceptance of persons. And, that there is no respect of persons with God, (the Apostle) says: "Is it possible by chance that he is God of the Jews only? and not of the Gentiles? (567) Yes! also of the Gentiles! Since it is the same God who justifies the circumcision by faith, and the uncircumcision through the same faith" And again: "Those whom he has called, not only from among the Jews, but also from among the Gentiles" And elsewhere: "There is one Lord, and one faith, and one baptism, and one God above all, and in the midst of all, and in every way (568)." 8. Return to Predestination propter praeuisa merita. To return to it (569): before the event, in the threat of captivity that he was already brandishing (570) against the people, he committed himself thus by oath: "I, I have spoken! now I will do! and I will not make (my word) turn back! And not for all that, was this will his own, but rather it was their impiety that brought him to that which precisely he did not want. And if, in the same resistance, after the threats, they had not wickedly remained, he would not have delivered them [170] into the hands" of enemies. He would have deemed it better to retract his own words rather than deliver them into the hands of foreign nations; as, once he had set his gaze upon the repentance of the Ninevites, he did not destroy the city Of Jeremiah also in his turn, in the anticipatory vision he has of him, he says: "Before you were formed in the womb (of your mother), I knew you" "I knew you," he says, to show that before (he existed), he knew what he was to be. And by an affixed seal he placed him among the saints, through the effect of what he says: "Before you came forth from the womb, I sanctified you, and I gave you (to be) a prophet to the nations" Likewise, also of Samuel and of John and of Paul (571), as indeed he (Paul) says expressly (572): "He chose me from my mother's womb to proclaim the Gospel of his Son through me and of the saints, of all absolutely (573), as the Apostle says: "Those whom he knew beforehand, he predestined to become conformed to the image of his Son" and again: "He chose us [171] before the world came into existence 9. In God the will is desirous only of good things. And that it is of good things that the will in God is desirous, Our Lord teaches, when he says formally: "This is the will of my Father, who is in heaven, that whoever has seen the Son and has believed in him, be fully (574) in possession of eternal life And again: "My food, here it is: that I fully (574) do the will of my Father" And: "This is the will of my Father, that of all that he has given me I lose absolutely (574) nothing, but that I fully (574) raise it up on the last day He wills therefore that all those who believe in the Son should not perish, but rather that they should rise on the day of the resurrection. And the work of the resurrection, it is after many generations, in the future, that it is to take place. Notwithstanding, God, this will for the good, both in the past he had it, by his nature, and in the present he has it. And the will is not accidental (575) for him, but natural, in conformity with his ineffable beneficence. 10. God also wills that his rational creatures be enamored of the good - Hence his economy. And, as previously (576) we have said, his will is perpetually in appetite for the good, and he wills that they also, his rational creatures creatures, come to be enamored of [172] good and workers of truth (577). Only, because he knows that half walk according to his will, and half do not walk, for this reason, the virtue of (those of) the (first) half, with the intention of stimulating the others in emulation for the good, from the maternal womb, he announces it; likewise, the unfitness (for good) of the others too; not as if he himself, such, from the maternal womb, unfit (for good) were to fashion, and such, fit! And if things were thus, why should he who is fit (for good) be praised, and he who is unfit be blamed, if it is he himself (God) who is creator of the quality that makes fit (for good) and of that which makes unfit for it? Moreover, there should be no accused person of this unfitness (for good), if it were (God) himself who had thus fashioned him from the maternal womb! And therefore, it is evident that, for God, to say: "Jacob, I loved; and Esau, I hated" is, in advance, to know that the former (578) must, in the future, be worthy of love because of his moral conduct, and the latter, hateful. And that (579) it is indeed because of his moral conduct that Esau became hateful, the apostle says: "Let no one (be) a fornicator [173] and impure like Esau!"; and: "No root of bitterness that, once above (the ground) it has appeared (580), should come to trouble the others" and he has made it evident that it is by his will that Esau became such, and not from God's fashioning. As elsewhere again (Scripture) says: "God created man upright, and it is they who, in the exercise of their thought, have devised evil (581)." And, through the prophet (God) says: "I planted you a "delightful vine", and how have you turned to bitterness, you, "vine fallen fallow"" Conclusion to I (Problem of evil and of freedom) and to II (Free evil and divine foreknowledge) and transition to the corollaries. [II. XVIII] Whence it appears that God, the work (which is each one) of all (beings), he works it beautiful! And to turn toward goodness and badness, it is the free will that he created having power to do this voluntarily, so that (582), whichever side he may come to desire, he might have power to turn; and that, according to his works, he might receive the retribution also corresponding to them; and he should by no means behave like an animal, for whom good works do not exist at all, nor hope of retribution, because it is formally an animal and does not know how to discern evil from good [174] by thought, but rather by virtue of the ways of behaving placed in its nature, by which, through memory (583) only, it is led toward the useful, and, by repulsion, turns away from the harmful. COROLLARIES TO I AND TO II ON THAT WHICH IS OUTSIDE FREEDOM 1. Instincts of Beasts. In them (the animals) there are also certain natural instincts, thanks to which, of certain future things, premonition, on occasion, comes to them. As in the horse (instinct in testing the ground) of the hoof, to know where to place the foot (584); and in the ox, in winter, on the side of the hollows (of the stable), to sit down, and, at the approach of spring, on the side of the doors, to turn back (584); and in the swallow, before autumn, to warm countries, to winter quarters, to depart; and in the crane, to know (585) in advance harsh winters, and, quickly, toward grassy places, to make haste; and in the jays, more quickly, in troops, to wheel about; and in the wood-pigeons, in gathering together, flights by flights, into a single band, to give themselves movement; and to the crows, leaving shaded places, as quickly as possible, to come to warm spots; and to the vultures, from afar, should a corpse be falling, to fix their gaze upon it; and to the others, and to all animals and birds. As for the ant, she too, from summer, to prepare [175] food; and to cut the grain in two, so that it may not germinate; and, in warm weather, to drag the millet out of the burrow, and to dry it; and to the bees, to plug the entrances of their hives before the cold with perforated wax (586); and to the bear, before winter, to enter the den; and to the wild beast, early, leaving the mountains, to move swiftly to the plain; and to the stags, to know precisely the mating season; and to the onagers, overcome with jealousy, to castrate the male young at a tender age. And all these instinctive knowledges are natural in animals, and not the fruit of reflective thought; (knowledges) which, by their creator, have been implanted in them, so that they incline by inclination toward the useful, and, by repulsion, distance themselves from things that can harm them. 2. Instincts of Men. [II. XIX] And not only in animals are instinctive knowledges thus natural, but also in men, who have language and intelligence.
